Worked examples 10.3 · Read a minimum and work backwards

First minimum and central width

A 600 nm laser passes through a 150 µm slit. The screen is 2.00 m away. Find the first dark angle, its distance from the centre and the central bright width.

Use equations (16), (5), (17)

\(a\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=1,2,\ldots\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)\(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\approx\frac{2\lambda L}{a},\quad \Omega=2\theta_1\)

Use (16) with \(m=1\): \(\sin\theta_1=(600\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm m)/(150\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm m)=0.00400\). Thus \(\theta_1\approx0.229^\circ\). From (5), \(x_1=2.00\tan\theta_1\approx8.00\ \mathrm{mm}\). Equation (17) gives \(W_0\approx16.0\ \mathrm{mm}\).

Working back from two minima

In another setup a 500 nm laser gives first minima 12.0 mm apart on a screen 1.50 m away. Find the slit width.

Use equations (17), (16), (5)

\(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\approx\frac{2\lambda L}{a},\quad \Omega=2\theta_1\)\(a\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=1,2,\ldots\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)

From (17), the one-sided distance is \(x_1=6.00\ \mathrm{mm}\) and \(x_1/L=0.00400\). Use the small-angle form of (16) and (5) to find \(a\approx\lambda L/x_1=125\ \mathrm{\mu m}\).

Worked example 10.3 · Read the graph first

Identify the first minima

The bottom graph has a = 10λ. The screen is 0.80 m away. Read both first-minimum angles, then estimate the angular and screen widths.

Use equations (17)

\(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\approx\frac{2\lambda L}{a},\quad \Omega=2\theta_1\)

Use equation (17), \(\Omega=2\theta_1\) and \(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\). The graph gives \(\theta_1\approx6^\circ\), so \(\Omega\approx12^\circ\) and \(W_0\approx2(0.80\,\mathrm m)\tan6^\circ\approx0.17\,\mathrm m\).

Worked examples 10.4 · From line density to screen position

600 nm laser through a 500 lines/mm grating

A screen is 1.00 m away. Find the angle and position of the first order, then decide if order 4 can reach a forward screen.

Use equations (18), (19), (5), (20)

\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)\(0<\Delta r=d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda<d\)

Equation (18) gives \(d=2.00\ \mathrm{\mu m}\). From (19), \(\sin\theta_1=0.300\), so \(\theta_1=17.46^\circ\). From (5), \(x_1=1.00\tan(17.46^\circ)\approx31.4\ \mathrm{cm}\). Order 4 requires \(4\lambda/d=1.20\), which fails (20). The highest order is 3, giving seven fringes on a sufficiently wide screen.

Read a scale using the centre coordinate

The central fringe reads 50.0 cm and first order reads 70.0 cm. The screen is 1.00 m away and the grating has 300 lines/mm. Find λ.

Use equations (14), (5), (18), (19)

\(D=|x_2-x_1|\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)

Equation (14) gives \(x_1=20.0\ \mathrm{cm}\). Equation (5) gives \(\tan\theta_1=0.200\), hence \(\sin\theta_1\approx0.1961\). From (18) and (19), \(\lambda=d\sin\theta_1\approx654\ \mathrm{nm}\).

Worked examples 10.4 · Complete spectra and overlapping lines

White light, 400–700 nm

A 500 lines/mm grating is 1.00 m from a sufficiently wide screen. Find the highest complete order and the width of the first-order spectrum on one side.

Use equations (18), (20), (19), (5), (14)

\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)\(0<\Delta r=d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda<d\)\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)\(D=|x_2-x_1|\)

Equation (18) gives \(d=2000\ \mathrm{nm}\). From (20), \(m<2000/700\approx2.86\), so order 2 is the last complete one. For order 1, (19) gives \(\sin\theta_{400}=0.200\) and \(\sin\theta_{700}=0.350\). From (5), the edges are at 20.4 and 37.4 cm, giving a width of about 17.0 cm.

Two colours at the same position

The second order of 600 nm light coincides with the third order of another colour on the same grating. Find that wavelength.

Use equations (19)

\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)

From (19), both colours share \(d\) and \(\theta\), so \(2(600\ \mathrm{nm})=3\lambda\). Hence \(\lambda=400\ \mathrm{nm}\).

Sentence Bank
S01 the central bright fringe is order zero
S02 the waves must maintain a constant phase difference
S03 the number of fringes equals the number of slits
S04 the first dark fringe is order zero

Laser light passing through two slits produces a stable overlap pattern because the emerging sources are A1coherent. At a screen point the two routes may have a A2path difference, producing either a A3bright fringe or a A4dark fringe according to reinforcement or cancellation.

For two initially in-phase sources, the central-axis point has equal path lengths and is the A5central bright fringe; A6the central bright fringe is order zero.

S01 count the intervals between fringe centres
S02 the small-angle equation works everywhere
S03 convert lengths to consistent units
S04 six bright centres span six intervals

In the small-angle model, B1fringe spacing grows when B2wavelength grows or B3slit-to-screen distance grows, but shrinks when B4slit separation grows.

When a ruler spans several fringes, B5count the intervals between fringe centres. In inverse calculations, B6convert lengths to consistent units.

Problem 3 · Reading a ruler and counting intervals

A scaled model shows six bright centres at 12.5, 15.5, 18.5, 21.5, 24.5 and 27.5 mm.

B. Use the first-to-last distance to find one fringe spacing.

Methods and answers

A. How many adjacent intervals do six consecutive bright centres span?

Use equation (13)

Six centres span five intervals, since an interval is a gap between adjacent centres.

B. Use the first-to-last distance to find one fringe spacing.

Use equation (13)

Divide by five, not six.

C. Find the separation between a bright centre and its immediately adjacent dark fringe.

Use equations (8), (9)

In the ideal model, a dark fringe lies midway between adjacent bright centres.

Problem 4 · Phase difference, angle and separation between fringes

Based on IPST chapter-end Problem 6 and Challenge Problems 2 and 4 · Teacher’s guide pp. 155, 163–165

A. Light of wavelength 500 nm passes through a double slit. The screen is 1.20 m away. At a point 15.0 mm from the centre, the phase difference is 6π radians. Estimate the slit separation in micrometres.

A. Light of wavelength 500 nm passes through a double slit. The screen is 1.20 m away. At a point 15.0 mm from the centre, the phase difference is 6π radians. Estimate the slit separation in micrometres.

Use equations (1), (6)

Problem 6 · Single slit · Separate screen width and angular width

A 500 nm laser passes through a 125 µm slit and the screen is 1.00 m away. Find the first dark angle, central angular width and separation of the two first minima on the screen.

Use equations (16), (17)

\(a\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=1,2,\ldots\)\(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\approx\frac{2\lambda L}{a},\quad \Omega=2\theta_1\)

Equation (16) gives \(\sin\theta_1=(500\times10^{-9})/(125\times10^{-6})=0.00400\), so \(\theta_1=0.229^\circ\). By (17), the angular width is \(2\theta_1=0.458^\circ\) and the screen width is \(2(1.00\ \mathrm m)\tan\theta_1\approx8.00\ \mathrm{mm}\).

0.229° · 0.458° · 8.00 mm

Problem 7 · Grating · Complete spectra and coincident lines

A 500 lines/mm grating receives 420–680 nm light and the screen is sufficiently wide. Find the highest complete order and the third-order wavelength that coincides with second-order 630 nm light.

Use equations (18), (20), (19)

\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)\(0<\Delta r=d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda<d\)\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)

Equation (18) gives \(d=2000\ \mathrm{nm}\). Test \(\lambda_{\max}=680\ \mathrm{nm}\) in (20): \(m<2000/680\approx2.94\), so order 2 is the last complete one. By (19), the same position gives the same \(d\sin\theta\), so \(\lambda=2(630\ \mathrm{nm})/3=420\ \mathrm{nm}\).

order 2 · 420 nm

Past question 1 · 2023 A-Level Physics (64) · Set 1, Question 11

In a double-slit interference experiment, a group of students investigates how the distance x from the centre of the central bright fringe to the centre of the first-order bright fringe depends on the slit separation d. They proceed as follows.

  1. Prepare three double-slit plates with different values of d, a green laser pointer, and a screen. Place the screen 2.0 metres from the double-slit plate.
  2. Direct the laser at right angles to the first plate, which has the smallest d. Measure x on the screen and record the reading.
  3. Repeat with the remaining plates in order of increasing d.
  4. Analyse the data and draw a conclusion.

Consider the following statements.

  1. A. The recorded value of x gives the position of a point of destructive interference.
  2. B. With a double-slit plate of d = 100 μm, x is greater than with a plate of d = 250 μm.
  3. C. If the students hypothesise that “as d increases, x also increases,” this experiment can be used to test their hypothesis.

Which statements are correct?

  1. A only
  2. B only
  3. C only
  4. A and C only
  5. B and C only

Original Set 1 paper (public scan) · Exam page 18, Question 11

Choice ______ Explain your reasoning for each statement before revealing the solution.

Use equations (2), (7), (9)

\(\Delta r=m\lambda\)\(x_{\mathrm B,m}\approx\frac{m\lambda L}{d}\)\(\Delta x\approx\frac{\lambda L}{d}\)

Choice 5 (statements B and C)

A is false. The centre of the first bright fringe is a point of constructive interference.

B is true. With the same light and screen distance, the centre-to-centre distance from the central bright fringe to the first bright fringe is inversely proportional to slit separation in the small-angle regime, by equation (9). It is larger at 100 µm than at 250 µm.

C is true. The procedure changes slit separation, keeps the light colour and screen distance fixed, and measures the position of the same fringe order. It can therefore test the stated hypothesis even though the predicted trend contradicts it.

See the explanation and correction for Question 11 from Physicsfree4TH

Wave optics 10.2–10.4Exit Ticket Revision · Answers

Past question 2 · 2025 A-Level Physics (64) · Set 1, Question 9

In an experiment, a student shines monochromatic light (light of a single frequency) through a double slit to study the interference pattern on a distant screen.

Question. Which adjustment should be made to increase the width of the central bright fringe?

  1. Move the screen farther from the slits.
  2. Use a double slit with greater slit separation.
  3. Use monochromatic light of a higher frequency.
  4. Both 1 and 2 are correct.
  5. Both 1 and 3 are correct.

Original myTCAS exam · Page 8, Question 9

Choice ______ Explain the effect of each adjustment before revealing the solution.

Use equations (15), (11)

\(W_0=2x_{\mathrm D,1}\approx\frac{\lambda L}{d}\approx\Delta x\)\(v=f\lambda\)

Choice 1. Move the screen farther away while keeping the light and slit separation fixed. By equation (15), this widens the central bright fringe.

Increasing slit separation with the other variables fixed narrows the fringe. Increasing frequency in the same medium reduces wavelength, which also narrows the fringe.

Past question 3 · Common Subjects 2564 · Physics (49) · Question 11

Light of wavelength 600 nanometres is incident normally on a double slit whose slit separation is 0.050 millimetres. The interference pattern is observed on a screen. The same light is then used with a single slit in place of the double slit. The first dark fringe in both patterns occurs at the same distance from the central bright fringe.

What is the width of the single slit, in metres?

  1. \(1.0\times10^{-4}\)
  2. \(5.0\times10^{-5}\)
  3. \(2.5\times10^{-5}\)
  4. \(1.4\times10^{-8}\)
  5. \(7.2\times10^{-9}\)

Source edition

Use equations (3), (4), (16)

\(\Delta r=(m-\tfrac12)\lambda\)\(\Delta r\approx d\sin\theta\)\(a\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=1,2,\ldots\)

Use (3), (4) and (16). The same displacement on the same screen gives the same angle. The double-slit minimum satisfies \(d\sin\theta=\lambda/2\); the single-slit minimum satisfies \(a\sin\theta=\lambda\). Dividing gives \(a=2d=2(0.050\ \mathrm{mm})=1.0\times10^{-4}\ \mathrm{m}\). Option 1.

Past question 4 · A-Level 2567 · Physics · Question 11 (Drive compilation)

A grating has 4,000 slits per centimetre. A narrow laser beam with wavelength 550 nanometres is incident normally on the grating and reaches a screen. Find the highest diffraction order of the bright fringes on the screen. (A-Level#67)

  1. Order 2
  2. Order 3
  3. Order 4
  4. Order 5
  5. Order 6

Source edition

Use equations (18), (20)

\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)\(0<\Delta r=d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda<d\)

By (18), \(d=(1\ \mathrm{cm})/4000=2.50\ \mathrm{\mu m}\). From (20), \(m<2.50/0.550\approx4.55\), so the highest order is 4. Order 5 would require \(\sin\theta>1\). Option 3.

  4. 10
  5. 11

\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)\(0<\Delta r=d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda<d\)

By (18), \(d=(1\ \mathrm{cm})/2500=4.0\ \mathrm{\mu m}\). Equations (19)–(20) give \(m<4.0/0.720\approx5.56\), so the highest order is 5. Five on the left, one in the centre and five on the right give 11 fringes. Official answer: option 5.

Past question 6 · A-Level 2569 · Physics (64) · Set 1 Question 27

A student shines red light of wavelength 600 nanometres through a grating with 1,000 slits per centimetre. The central bright line and the first- and second-order bright lines appear on a screen 1.00 metres away at the positions shown. Treat the screen as very distant compared with the slit separation. Find the distance x in centimetres.

Source edition · Official answer

Use equations (18), (19), (5), (14)

\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)\(D=|x_2-x_1|\)

Equation (18) gives \(d=(1\ \mathrm{cm})/1000=1.00\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm m\). By (19) with \(m=2\), \(\sin\theta_2=2(600\times10^{-9})/(1.00\times10^{-5})=0.120\) or \(\theta_2\approx6.89^\circ\). In the small-angle form of (5), the one-sided distance is \(L\sin\theta_2=12.0\ \mathrm{cm}\). The arrow joins the two second-order maxima, giving the official answer \(x=24.00\ \mathrm{cm}\). Using \(\tan\theta_2\) directly gives about 24.17 cm.

Knowledge Recall · Double slit

Write each equation from memory. Add its condition when one is required.

Phase from path difference

\(\Delta\phi=2\pi\frac{\Delta r}{\lambda}\)
(1)

Bright condition for initially in-phase slits

\(\Delta r=m\lambda\)
(2)

Dark condition, numbering the first dark fringe 1

\(\Delta r=(m-\tfrac12)\lambda\)
(3)

Path difference for far-field geometry

\(\Delta r\approx d\sin\theta\)
(4)

Angle and screen coordinate

\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)
(5)

Double-slit path difference near the axis

\(\Delta r\approx\frac{dx}{L}\)
(6)

Double-slit bright position for small angles

\(x_{\mathrm B,m}\approx\frac{m\lambda L}{d}\)
(7)

Double-slit dark position for small angles

\(x_{\mathrm D,m}\approx(m-\tfrac12)\frac{\lambda L}{d}\)
(8)

Double-slit bright fringe spacing, for small angles

\(\Delta x\approx\frac{\lambda L}{d}\)
(9)

Spacing ratio with a wavelength change

\(\frac{\Delta x_2}{\Delta x_1}\approx\frac{\lambda_2}{\lambda_1}\frac{L_2}{L_1}\frac{d_1}{d_2}\)
(10)

Wave speed, frequency and wavelength

\(v=f\lambda\)
(11)

Spacing ratio with a frequency change at fixed wave speed

\(\frac{\Delta x_2}{\Delta x_1}\approx\frac{f_1}{f_2}\frac{L_2}{L_1}\frac{d_1}{d_2}\)
(12)

Spacing from several equally spaced intervals

\(\Delta x=\frac{|x_{\mathrm{last}}-x_{\mathrm{first}}|}{N-1}\)
(13)

Distance between two screen coordinates

\(D=|x_2-x_1|\)
(14)

Central double-slit width for small angles

\(W_0=2x_{\mathrm D,1}\approx\frac{\lambda L}{d}\approx\Delta x\)
(15)

Double slit · From the lab to interval counting

1. How does fringe spacing change when slit separation changes and other variables stay fixed?

Spacing decreases as \(d\) increases by (9), for small angles.

2. How many intervals lie between five consecutive bright fringes? Describe both ends of the span.

Four intervals, from the first bright centre to the fifth.

3. Which points bound the central bright width W₀?

Measure between the first minimum on the left and the first minimum on the right.

Knowledge Recall · Single slit and grating

Write each equation from memory. Add its condition when one is required.

Single-slit dark-fringe condition

\(a\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=1,2,\ldots\)
(16)

Central width on a screen and angular width

\(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\approx\frac{2\lambda L}{a},\quad \Omega=2\theta_1\)
(17)

Slit separation from line density

\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)
(18)

Grating principal bright orders

\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)
(19)

Off-axis orders on a forward screen

\(0<\Delta r=d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda<d\)
(20)

Single slit and grating · Explain and choose conditions

1. Why does a single slit give a first minimum? What happens to its central fringe when the slit narrows?

At the first minimum, pairs across the two halves differ in path by half a wavelength and cancel. Narrowing the slit broadens the centre at fixed wavelength and screen distance.

2. What colour is the centre for white light through a grating? Which wavelength should be checked first to see if the entire range is visible?

The centre is white because all wavelengths overlap. Check the longest wavelength because it diffracts the farthest at a fixed order.

3. What types of position do (16) and (19) locate? When can sin θ be approximated by tan θ?

Equation (16) locates single-slit minima; (19) locates principal grating maxima. The approximation requires a small angle.

Supplementary question 1 · What a fringe photograph does and does not tell us

Inspect the real experimental fringe photograph. No ruler or apparatus dimensions are provided, so use it to interpret the pattern, not infer a numerical wavelength.

A. Describe the visible pattern and explain the dark regions between bright fringes.

B. If the central point is equidistant from two initially in-phase slits, state its fringe type and order.

C. Name at least two additional quantities needed to obtain wavelength from a measured fringe spacing.

Methods and answers

A. Describe the visible pattern and explain the dark regions between bright fringes.

Alternating bright and dark regions occur; destructive interference of the two waves produces dark fringes at odd half-wavelength path differences.

B. If the central point is equidistant from two initially in-phase slits, state its fringe type and order.

Path difference is zero, so this is the central bright fringe, order 0.

C. Name at least two additional quantities needed to obtain wavelength from a measured fringe spacing.

Need slit-centre separation and slit-to-screen distance, plus a scaled measurement of fringe spacing.

Supplementary question 2 · Path difference and fringe order

Light wavelength is 520 nm; the two sources start in phase. The following path lengths are measured to point P.

A. If the two path lengths are 38.40 µm and 39.96 µm, find the path difference and identify the fringe.

Methods and answers

A. If the two path lengths are 38.40 µm and 39.96 µm, find the path difference and identify the fringe.

Use equation (2)

Choose bright (antinode): .

An allowed integer, hence the third bright fringe.

Supplementary question 3 · Double slit · Count ruler intervals

A simulation screenshot shows five consecutive bright fringes. The first centre is at 1.2 cm and the last at 3.2 cm. Draw a tick for each centre and find the mean spacing.

Use equations (14), (13)

\(D=|x_2-x_1|\)\(\Delta x=\frac{|x_{\mathrm{last}}-x_{\mathrm{first}}|}{N-1}\)

From (14), the span of the five centres is \(3.2-1.2=2.0\ \mathrm{cm}\). Five marks delimit four intervals, so (13) gives \(\Delta x=2.0\ \mathrm{cm}/4=0.50\ \mathrm{cm}\).

0.50 cm

Supplementary question 4 · Inverse wavelength and slit-separation calculations

Use the small-angle model for the following measurements.

A. Slit separation is 0.40 mm, screen distance 2.00 m and fringe spacing 2.75 mm. Find the wavelength in nanometres.

B. Wavelength 550 nm produces 5.50 mm spacing at 2.00 m. Find the slit separation.

C. The two minima bounding the central bright fringe are 5.50 mm apart. In the ideal double-slit model, is its width equal to the fringe spacing?

Methods and answers

A. Slit separation is 0.40 mm, screen distance 2.00 m and fringe spacing 2.75 mm. Find the wavelength in nanometres.

Use equation (9)

The result lies within the visible-light wavelength range.

B. Wavelength 550 nm produces 5.50 mm spacing at 2.00 m. Find the slit separation.

Use equation (9)

C. The two minima bounding the central bright fringe are 5.50 mm apart. In the ideal double-slit model, is its width equal to the fringe spacing?

Use equations (8), (9)

Yes. The first bounding minima are at ±Δx/2, so the central interference fringe is Δx = 5.50 mm wide. This is not the single-slit diffraction envelope width.

Supplementary question 10 · Single slit · Interpret evidence and predict

Use the IPST photograph of the 50, 100 and 200 µm slits. With the same laser and screen distance, the 100 µm slit gives a 10.0 mm central width. Predict the width for the 50 µm slit in the small-angle regime. Refer to both the image and an equation.

Use equations (17)

\(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\approx\frac{2\lambda L}{a},\quad \Omega=2\theta_1\)

The 50 µm pattern has a wider centre in the image. By (17) at fixed \(\lambda,L\), \(W_{0,50}/W_{0,100}\approx100/50=2\), so \(W_{0,50}\approx20.0\ \mathrm{mm}\). The numerical width comes from the given measurement; the image supports the trend.

about 20.0 mm

Supplementary question 11 · Double and single slit · Coincident minima

With the same laser and screen, a double slit of separation 180 µm has its second minimum at the same position as the first minimum of a single slit. Find the single-slit width and state both conditions.

Use equations (3), (4), (16)

\(\Delta r=(m-\tfrac12)\lambda\)\(\Delta r\approx d\sin\theta\)\(a\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=1,2,\ldots\)

The same position at the same \(L\) gives the same angle. By (3) and (4), the double slit gives \(d\sin\theta=(2-\tfrac12)\lambda\). By (16), the single slit gives \(a\sin\theta=\lambda\). Dividing the equations gives \(a=180\ \mathrm{\mu m}/1.5=120\ \mathrm{\mu m}\).

120 µm

Supplementary question 12 · Grating · Read two positions to find the wavelength

A screen 1.20 m away is used with a 400 lines/mm grating. First-order maxima on opposite sides are at 23.0 and 83.0 cm on a ruler. Find the centre, the one-sided distance and the wavelength using trigonometry.

Use equations (14), (5), (18), (19)

\(D=|x_2-x_1|\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)

By symmetry the centre is \((23.0+83.0)/2=53.0\ \mathrm{cm}\), and the one-sided distance is \(30.0\ \mathrm{cm}\). By (5), \(\tan\theta=0.300/1.20=0.250\), so \(\sin\theta=0.2425\). By (18), \(d=2.50\ \mathrm{\mu m}\). Equation (19) with \(m=1\) gives \(\lambda=d\sin\theta\approx606\ \mathrm{nm}\).

53.0 cm · 30.0 cm · 606 nm

Supplementary question 13 · Grating · An order parallel to the screen

A 500 lines/mm grating is illuminated with a wavelength exactly one-third of the slit separation. On a sufficiently wide forward screen perpendicular to the central ray, find the largest order that hits the screen and the number of principal maxima.

An electromagnetic wave of wavelength 650 nanometres is incident normally on a grating. The central bright fringe and first-order fringes appear on a screen 1 metre from the grating, as shown.

Consider the following statements.

Bring your evidence to the board discussion

Students chosen at random log their readings and handwrite the conclusions on the teacher’s iPad. Point to the two readings that support each group conclusion.

Conclusions from the experiment

  • With the same light and screen distance, greater slit separation produces narrower bright fringes.
  • With the same slits and screen distance, red light has a longer wavelength and produces wider fringes than green light.
  • With the same light and slits, a more distant screen produces wider fringes.

When light from two slits overlaps, why do bright and dark fringes form? How can we explain the three trends using path difference?

The two slits; the slit midpoint, screen centre and observation point, respectively.
Path lengths and . is the magnitude of their difference, in metres.
Slit centre separation and perpendicular slit-to-screen distance, in metres.
Coordinate of P from C, and angle between OP and axis OC. Start on the positive-coordinate side.
Wavelength (metres) and magnitude of the phase difference caused by unequal paths (radians).

From path difference to phase difference

Successive crests are separated by \(\lambda\), an accumulated phase difference of \(2\pi\) radians. They are in phase. Apply this proportion to the optical path difference \(\Delta r\).

(1)

Illuminating both openings from one source maintains equal frequency and a constant phase difference: coherent sources. The following conditions use initially in-phase light. Let denote the fringe order for the chosen condition.

Bright: reinforcement occurs at an integer number of wavelengths; .

(2)

Dark: cancellation occurs at an odd half-integer number of wavelengths; .

(3)

The central bright fringe has order 0; the first dark fringe has order 1. Complete darkness also requires equal arriving amplitudes.

5 · Ratios and explanations of the experiment

Subscripts 1 and 2 denote before and after. Use \(W_0\approx\Delta x\) for the near-axis simulation measurements.

In the sample, A to B at 1 m doubles the slit separation and the width falls from 10.64 to 5.32 mm. For B at 1 and 2 m, doubling the screen distance increases the width from 5.32 to 10.64 mm.

Finding, with other factors fixedExplanation
Greater slit separation narrows the fringes.Slit separation is in the denominator. Increasing it decreases fringe spacing.
Longer wavelength widens the fringes.Wavelength is in the numerator. Increasing it increases fringe spacing.
A more distant screen widens the fringes.Screen distance is in the numerator. Increasing it increases fringe spacing.

Increasing intensity alone makes fringes brighter, while their positions and spacing stay unchanged in this model.

Bright/dark conditions (2) and (3) assume initially in-phase light. Opposite initial phases swap the conditions. Check the starting phase before choosing an equation.

10.3 · Can one slit produce dark fringes?

Watch the MIT OpenCourseWare single-slit demonstration

Find the dark regions on each side of the central maximum. Compare widths qualitatively from this figure, which has no labelled screen-distance scale.

Class notes · What happens as the slit gets narrower at fixed wavelength and screen distance?

The central bright fringe widens and the side fringes spread farther out.

Class notes · How can light from different parts of one slit cancel on the screen?

Points across the opening act as secondary wave sources. At some screen positions their path differences lead to cancellation.

Kru Nix CenturyPage 30 / 42
Wave optics 10.2–10.4Exit Ticket Revision · Answers

Deriving the single-slit minima

Let \(a\) be the slit width and \(\theta_m\) the angle to dark order \(m\). For normal illumination and a distant screen, choose two points separated by \(a/2\) as shown.

For the first minimum, pairs \(a/2\) apart must have path difference \(\Delta r=\lambda/2\). From the triangle, \(\sin\theta_1=\frac{\Delta r}{a/2}=\frac{\lambda/2}{a/2}=\frac{\lambda}{a}\). The pairs cancel and \(a\sin\theta_1=\lambda\).

Class notes · Why is the on-axis fringe bright?

At zero angle, the contributions from the whole slit have zero path difference and add constructively.

Second and third minima and side maxima

Pair adjacent parts, such as A and A′. Matching points are \(a/4\) apart at minimum 2 and \(a/6\) apart at minimum 3. Each pair cancels when the path difference is \(\lambda/2\).

\(\sin\theta_2=\frac{\lambda/2}{a/4}=\frac{2\lambda}{a}\)

\(\sin\theta_3=\frac{\lambda/2}{a/6}=\frac{3\lambda}{a}\)

For dark order \(m\), use \(2m\) equal parts. Each pair is \(a/(2m)\) apart and its path difference is \(\lambda/2\).

\(a\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=1,2,\ldots\)
(16)

Use the minima to bound each side maximum

Position on one sideMinimum 1Side maximum 1Minimum 2Side maximum 2Minimum 3
\(a\sin\theta\)\(\lambda\)Between\(2\lambda\)Between\(3\lambda\)

On an intensity plot, a dark fringe touches zero and a side maximum lies between two minima.

Wave optics 10.2–10.4Exit Ticket Revision · Answers

Reading intensity and finding central width

Read the first zeros on the left and right. For \(a=5\lambda\) they are about \(\pm12^\circ\), and for \(a=10\lambda\) about \(\pm6^\circ\). The horizontal axis is in degrees, giving dark-to-dark widths of about 24° and 12°, respectively. The arrow at intensity 0.5 marks the width at half the maximum intensity.

On a screen the first minima are at \(x=\pm L\tan\theta_1\). The central width \(W_0\) spans both first minima. For small angles, \(\frac{ax_1}{L}\approx\lambda\) at the first minimum. Thus \(x_1\approx\frac{\lambda L}{a}\); double it for the width.

\(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\approx\frac{2\lambda L}{a},\quad \Omega=2\theta_1\)
(17)

Wave optics 10.2–10.4Exit Ticket Revision · Answers

10.4 · From two slits to a diffraction grating

With wavelength, slit width and slit separation fixed, more illuminated slits narrow the principal peaks at the same angles. A grating has many parallel, equally spaced slits or grooves.

Class notes · How do the centre and sides change when white light replaces the laser?

All wavelengths overlap at the centre, forming white light. Off-axis orders separate into spectra.

Kru Nix CenturyPage 33 / 42
Wave optics 10.2–10.4Exit Ticket Revision · Answers

Slit separation, bright orders and the highest order

If the line density is \(N\) slits per unit length, the centre-to-centre separation is its reciprocal. For example, 500 lines/mm gives \(d=(1\ \mathrm{mm})/500=2.00\ \mathrm{\mu m}\).

\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)
(18)

For normal illumination the slit waves start in phase. In the far field the path difference between neighbours is \(d\sin\theta_m\). An integer multiple of \(\lambda\) puts all the slit contributions in phase.

\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)
(19)

The centre is \(m=0\). Off-axis fringes on a forward screen require \(0<\theta_m<90^\circ\). If \(m\lambda=d\), the ray is parallel to the screen and does not meet it at a finite position.

\(0<\Delta r=d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda<d\)
(20)

At a fixed positive order, a longer wavelength lies farther from the centre. Use \(\lambda_{\max}\) to check whether a whole spectrum fits. Coincident colours have the same \(m\lambda\).

Wave optics 10.2–10.4Exit Ticket Revision · Answers

Double-slit examples 1–2

Example 1 · Identify orders

Light has wavelength 500 nanometres. Classify path differences of 1500 and 1250 nanometres.

Use equations (2), (3)

1500 nanometres · test bright (antinode)

: an allowed integer → third bright fringe.

1250 nanometres · test dark (node)

: an integer at least 1 → third dark fringe.

Testing the second case with the bright formula gives , not an integer: there is no bright order 2.5.

Method: choose a bright or dark condition, rearrange for order, and verify an integer in its allowed range.

Example 2 · Accumulated and equivalent phase

For light of wavelength 600 nanometres, the paths are 65.00 and 65.60 centimetres. Find the phase difference.

Use equation (1)

This is 10000 complete cycles, equivalent to 0 rad (or 2π rad): constructive interference. The accumulated phase itself is not zero.

Method: subtract paths, convert to metres, then multiply cycles by 2π. Distinguish accumulated phase from equivalent phase.

. Equate positions and cancel the common factor L/d.

Double-slit examples 11–12

Example 11 · Same side or opposite sides?

Adjacent bright fringes are 4.0 millimetres apart. Find the separation of bright order 1 and dark order 2 on the same side and on opposite sides. Also find the separation of the two third-order bright fringes.

Put the origin at the screen centre, assign signs, and use the two-coordinate distance from page 6.

Reasoning Set the central bright fringe at zero. Coordinates are positive to the right and negative to the left. Take the absolute difference between the two coordinates.

Each spacing is 4.0 mm. Bright orders 1 and 3 are one and three spacings from the centre (4.0 and 12 mm). Dark order 2 is halfway between bright orders 1 and 2, or 1.5 spacings from the centre (6.0 mm).

Working · Use equation (14)

① Same side · Both fringes are on the right, so subtract two positive coordinates.

② Opposite sides · Bright order 1 is on the left, so subtract its negative coordinate.

③ Third bright fringe on each side · Equal distances from the centre, opposite signs.

Example 12 · Beyond small angles

Light has wavelength 600 nanometres, slit separation 8.0 micrometres and screen distance 0.50 metres. Locate the second bright fringe.

Use equations (2), (4), (5)

The small-angle result is 75.0 mm. Here use sin to find the angle, then tan for position. The distant-screen condition still applies.

Method: interference determines sine of the angle; screen geometry uses tangent. Find the angle before locating the fringe.

Worked examples 10.3 · Read a minimum and work backwards

First minimum and central width

A 600 nm laser passes through a 150 µm slit. The screen is 2.00 m away. Find the first dark angle, its distance from the centre and the central bright width.

Use equations (16), (5), (17)

\(a\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=1,2,\ldots\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)\(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\approx\frac{2\lambda L}{a},\quad \Omega=2\theta_1\)

Use (16) with \(m=1\): \(\sin\theta_1=(600\times10^{-9}\ \mathrm m)/(150\times10^{-6}\ \mathrm m)=0.00400\). Thus \(\theta_1\approx0.229^\circ\). From (5), \(x_1=2.00\tan\theta_1\approx8.00\ \mathrm{mm}\). Equation (17) gives \(W_0\approx16.0\ \mathrm{mm}\).

Working back from two minima

In another setup a 500 nm laser gives first minima 12.0 mm apart on a screen 1.50 m away. Find the slit width.

Use equations (17), (16), (5)

\(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\approx\frac{2\lambda L}{a},\quad \Omega=2\theta_1\)\(a\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=1,2,\ldots\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)

From (17), the one-sided distance is \(x_1=6.00\ \mathrm{mm}\) and \(x_1/L=0.00400\). Use the small-angle form of (16) and (5) to find \(a\approx\lambda L/x_1=125\ \mathrm{\mu m}\).

Worked example 10.3 · Read the graph first

Identify the first minima

The bottom graph has a = 10λ. The screen is 0.80 m away. Read both first-minimum angles, then estimate the angular and screen widths.

Use equations (17)

\(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\approx\frac{2\lambda L}{a},\quad \Omega=2\theta_1\)

Use equation (17), \(\Omega=2\theta_1\) and \(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\). The graph gives \(\theta_1\approx6^\circ\), so \(\Omega\approx12^\circ\) and \(W_0\approx2(0.80\,\mathrm m)\tan6^\circ\approx0.17\,\mathrm m\).

Worked examples 10.4 · From line density to screen position

600 nm laser through a 500 lines/mm grating

A screen is 1.00 m away. Find the angle and position of the first order, then decide if order 4 can reach a forward screen.

Use equations (18), (19), (5), (20)

\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)\(0<\Delta r=d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda<d\)

Equation (18) gives \(d=2.00\ \mathrm{\mu m}\). From (19), \(\sin\theta_1=0.300\), so \(\theta_1=17.46^\circ\). From (5), \(x_1=1.00\tan(17.46^\circ)\approx31.4\ \mathrm{cm}\). Order 4 requires \(4\lambda/d=1.20\), which fails (20). The highest order is 3, giving seven fringes on a sufficiently wide screen.

Read a scale using the centre coordinate

The central fringe reads 50.0 cm and first order reads 70.0 cm. The screen is 1.00 m away and the grating has 300 lines/mm. Find λ.

Use equations (14), (5), (18), (19)

\(D=|x_2-x_1|\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)

Equation (14) gives \(x_1=20.0\ \mathrm{cm}\). Equation (5) gives \(\tan\theta_1=0.200\), hence \(\sin\theta_1\approx0.1961\). From (18) and (19), \(\lambda=d\sin\theta_1\approx654\ \mathrm{nm}\).

Worked examples 10.4 · Complete spectra and overlapping lines

White light, 400–700 nm

A 500 lines/mm grating is 1.00 m from a sufficiently wide screen. Find the highest complete order and the width of the first-order spectrum on one side.

Use equations (18), (20), (19), (5), (14)

\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)\(0<\Delta r=d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda<d\)\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)\(D=|x_2-x_1|\)

Equation (18) gives \(d=2000\ \mathrm{nm}\). From (20), \(m<2000/700\approx2.86\), so order 2 is the last complete one. For order 1, (19) gives \(\sin\theta_{400}=0.200\) and \(\sin\theta_{700}=0.350\). From (5), the edges are at 20.4 and 37.4 cm, giving a width of about 17.0 cm.

Two colours at the same position

The second order of 600 nm light coincides with the third order of another colour on the same grating. Find that wavelength.

Use equations (19)

\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)

From (19), both colours share \(d\) and \(\theta\), so \(2(600\ \mathrm{nm})=3\lambda\). Hence \(\lambda=400\ \mathrm{nm}\).

Knowledge Recall · Double slit

Write each equation from memory. Add its condition when one is required.

Phase from path difference

\(\Delta\phi=2\pi\frac{\Delta r}{\lambda}\)
(1)

Bright condition for initially in-phase slits

\(\Delta r=m\lambda\)
(2)

Dark condition, numbering the first dark fringe 1

\(\Delta r=(m-\tfrac12)\lambda\)
(3)

Path difference for far-field geometry

\(\Delta r\approx d\sin\theta\)
(4)

Angle and screen coordinate

\(\tan\theta=\frac{x}{L}\)
(5)

Double-slit path difference near the axis

\(\Delta r\approx\frac{dx}{L}\)
(6)

Double-slit dark position for small angles

(10)

Wave speed, frequency and wavelength

\(v=f\lambda\)
(11)

(15)

Double slit · From the lab to interval counting

1. How does fringe spacing change when slit separation changes and other variables stay fixed?

Spacing decreases as \(d\) increases by (9), for small angles.

2. How many intervals lie between five consecutive bright fringes? Describe both ends of the span.

Four intervals, from the first bright centre to the fifth.

3. Which points bound the central bright width W₀?

Measure between the first minimum on the left and the first minimum on the right.

Knowledge Recall · Single slit and grating

Write each equation from memory. Add its condition when one is required.

Single-slit dark-fringe condition

\(a\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=1,2,\ldots\)
(16)

Central width on a screen and angular width

\(W_0=2L\tan\theta_1\approx\frac{2\lambda L}{a},\quad \Omega=2\theta_1\)
(17)

Slit separation from line density

\(d=\frac{1}{N}\)
(18)

Grating principal bright orders

\(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda,\quad m=0,1,2,\ldots\)
(19)

Off-axis orders on a forward screen

\(0<\Delta r=d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda<d\)
(20)

Single slit and grating · Explain and choose conditions

1. Why does a single slit give a first minimum? What happens to its central fringe when the slit narrows?

At the first minimum, pairs across the two halves differ in path by half a wavelength and cancel. Narrowing the slit broadens the centre at fixed wavelength and screen distance.

2. What colour is the centre for white light through a grating? Which wavelength should be checked first to see if the entire range is visible?

The centre is white because all wavelengths overlap. Check the longest wavelength because it diffracts the farthest at a fixed order.

3. What types of position do (16) and (19) locate? When can sin θ be approximated by tan θ?

Equation (16) locates single-slit minima; (19) locates principal grating maxima. The approximation requires a small angle.

Kru Nix CenturyPage 2 / 2
